## Document 002: 1-8月财政收支数据解读：8月财政收入同比增速有所回落

Source PDF: all report pdf/1-8月财政收支数据解读：8月财政收入同比增速有所回落.pdf
Report source: 国新证券股份
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

市场研究部 2026年9月21日
事件
9 月 18 日，财政部发布，1-8 月，全国一般公共预算收入
156633亿元，同比增长 5.7%；全国一般公共预算支出 181451亿
元，同比增长 1.2%。
解读
一、1-8 月财政收入增速比 1-7 月小幅回落，8 月财政收入、
地方一般公共预算本级收入和税收收入增速比7月有所回落。
1-8月，全国一般公共预算收入 156633亿元，同比增长 5.7%，
比 1-7月回落 0.1个百分点，比 2025年同期提高 5.4个百分点。
其中，8 月，全国一般公共预算收入 12937 亿元，同比增长
4.7%，比 7月回落 7.0 个百分点，比 2025 年同期提高 2.7 个百分
点。
1-8月，全国税收收入同比增长 6.6%，比 1-7月回落 0.1个百
分点，比 2025年同期提高 6.6个百分点；非税收入同比增长 1.5%，
比 1-7月回落 0.1个百分点，与 2025年同期持平。
其中，8 月全国税收收入同比增长 5.6%，比 7 月回落 8.2 个

百分点，比 2025 年同期提高 2.2 个百分点；非税收入同比增长
0.3%，比 7月提高 5.7 个百分点，比 2025 年同期提高 4.1 个百分
点。
分中央和地方看，1-8 月，中央一般公共预算收入同比增长
9.1%，与 1-7月持平；地方一般公共预算本级收入同比增长 3.1%，
比 1-7月回落 0.2个百分点。
8月，中央一般公共预算收入同比增长 9.3%，比 7月回落 7.5
个百分点；地方一般公共预算本级收入同比增长 0.7%，比 7 月回
落 6.1个百分点。
二、8 月国内增值税、国内消费税、企业所得税、出口退税、
个人所得税、证券交易印花税等主体税种增速均比7月回落。
1-8 月，国内增值税 50179 亿元，同比增长 5.9%，比 1-7 月
回落 0.2 个百分点。其中，8 月同比增长 4.0%， 比 7 月回落 2.7
个百分点。
宏观研究
8月财政收入同比增速有所回落
——1-8月财政收支数据解读
市场表现截至 2026.8.31

分析师：郝大明

三、1-8 月全国财政支出小幅增长，比 1-7 月小幅回落，8 月
同比小幅下降。
1-8月，全国一般公共预算支出 181451亿元，同比增长 1.2%，
比 1-7月回落 0.1个百分点。
分中央和地方看，中央一般公共预算本级支出 28177 亿元，
同比增长 6%，比 1-7 月回落 0.2 个百分点；地方一般公共预算支
出 153274亿元，同比增长 0.3%，比 1-7月回落 0.2个百分点。
1-8月，财政支出增速较高的是，卫生健康支出同比增长 9.2%，
比 1-7月回落 0.6个百分点，社会保障支出同比增长 6.5%，比 1-7
月回落 0.5 个百分点，债务付息支出同比增长 5.4%，比 1-7 月提
高 0.2个百分点，科学技术支出同比增长 2.2%，比 1-7月提高 0.7

个百分点，教育支出同比增长 0.7%，与 1-7月持平。
其余支出 1-8 月均同比下降，其中，农林水支出同比下降
6.0%，节能环保支出同比下降 12.3%，文化旅游支出同比下降
7.2%，交通运输支出同比下降 2.9%，城乡社区事务支出同比下降
3.0%。
其中，8 月，全国一般公共预算支出同比下降 0.1%，比 7 月
回落 0.6 个百分点，其中，中央支出同比增长 4.7%，比 7 月提高

## Document 012: 2026-09-18_［みずほ経済EXPRESS］みずほGDPナウ（2026年9月中旬時点）_4b5dfb76

みずほ経済 EXPRESS

みずほ GDP ナウ（2026 年 9 月中旬時点） 
～7～9 月期 GDP は前期比＋0.54％（年率＋2.17％）と推計～

調査部  シニア日本経済エコノミスト 越山祐資 
同             エコノミスト  阿部大樹 
080-1069-4835 yusuke.koshiyama@mizuho-rt.co.jp 
■ 9 月中旬時点の「みずほ GDP ナウ」は 7～9 月期 GDP を前期比＋0.54％と予測 
みずほ総合研究所では景気動向をタイムリーに把握するべく、浦沢（2023）等を参考にGDPナウキ
ャスティングに取り組んできた。これを「みずほGDPナウ」と呼称することとし、太田他（2024）で
はデータドリブン予測である「みずほGDPナウ」が民間予測平均並みの予測精度を確保できることを
示した。その上で、酒井他（2024）をはじめとして、月次経済指標を用いたGDPナウキャスティング
の結果を紹介してきたところである。 
本稿では9月中旬時点までに得られる月次経済指標を用い、 「みずほGDPナウ」 による7～9月期GDP
の予測結果を紹介する。予測に用いたデータは、7月分の鉱工業生産、 消費活動指数、 所定外労働時間、
消費財出荷指数、第3次産業活動指数、8月分の中小企業景況調査（売上げ見通しDI）である1。図表1
には、予測に使用した各月次経済指標の推移を示している。 
図表 1 各月次経済指標の推移

（注）青字は改善、赤字は悪化を示す

日本銀行「消費活動指数」より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

モデルによる9 月中旬時点における7 ～9月期実質GDP成長率の予測値は、 図表2のとおり前期比＋
0.54％（年率＋2.17％）と、高い伸びを示す結果になった。他機関による予測では、東京財団政策研究
所による9月14日時点のナウキャスティングが同＋0.98％（年率＋3.99％）と、大幅プラスを予測して
いる。一方、日本経済研究センターが公表した9月のESPフォーキャスト調査（回答期間：2026年9月2
日～9月9日、 回答者37名） における民間予測値平均は前期比年率＋0.25％と、 「みずほGDPナ ウ 」の 予
測値対比で小幅な伸びにとどまっている。7～9月期実質GDP予測値については、データドリブン予測
値と民間コンセンサスが大きく乖離していると言えよう。 
両者の乖離は、景気判断の違いというよりも、GDP統計上のテクニカル要因による部分が大きいと
みられる。4～6月期には、中東情勢悪化を背景に原油・ナフサ輸入が大幅に減少したほか、石油化学
製品の供給途絶懸念から幅広い企業で在庫積み増しの動きが生じた。やや直感に反するが、いずれも
GDPに対しては押し上げ要因となる。 そのため、4～6月期GDPは前期比＋0.36％ （年率＋1.45％） とプ
ラス成長になったものの、成長率が示すほど景気の実態は強くなかったとみるのが自然であろう。反
対に、7～9月期については、4～6月期の反動で輸入増・在庫減の動きが顕在化し、GDPには一定の下
方圧力がかかる可能性がある。多くの民間エコノミストが保守的な見方をしているのは、こうしたテ
クニカルな要因が考慮されているためだと考えられる。なお、みずほ総合研究所でも、7～9月期GDP
は横ばい圏の伸びにとどまるとみている。 詳細については、 服部直樹 「年率＋1.4％と1次速報から小幅
に上方修正（4～6月期2次QE）」（2026年9月8日） を参照されたい。  
■ 景気実態は改善方向。今後民間予測値が上方修正される可能性にも留意 
データドリブン予測はテクニカル要因を反映しにくい傾向がある反面、より景気実態に近い動きを
捉えているとみられる。 「みずほGDPナウ」 による7～9月期のGDP（1次速報値） の予測にあたっては、
9月中旬までに公表された月次経済指標を使って予測値を2回更新した。 図表3では、予測値の更新過程
と各月次経済指標の寄与度を示している2。予測値上昇の主因は、8月の中小企業景況調査（売上げ見
図表 2 各機関の実質GDP成長率予測値 図表 3 予測値の改定過程と各経済指標の寄与度

キャスティング」、日本経済研究センター「ESPフォーキャス
ト調査」等より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

寄与度を示す。「みずほGDPナウ」は各経済指標の結果を公表
前に予測しており （モデル予測値） 、 寄与度はモデル予測値と

3

通しDI）が高水準となったことである。要因としては、中東危機に起因する供給網混乱の緩和や旺盛
なAI・データセンター関連需要が企業活動を押し上げていることが考えられる。また、鉱工業生産指
数も予測値の押し上げに寄与した。やはり、半導体製造装置などのAI・データセンター関連製品が好
調であるほか、中東情勢悪化の影響で3・4月に落ち込んだ石油化学製品の回復傾向が続いている。 
以上のように、原油やナフサの輸入増、企業の在庫調整といった動きから、GDP統計上の7～9月期
成長率は低い伸びに抑えられるとみている。ただし、「みずほGDPナウ」が示すとおり、足元の景気
実態は4～6月期対比で緩やかな改善傾向にある。総じて企業部門の回復を示す指標が多く、特に鉱工
業生産は8月の生産予測指数（補正値ベース）が前月比＋3.1％となるなど、好調の持続を示唆してい
る。石油化学製品における在庫復元の動きが続く可能性も考慮すれば、民間エコノミストによるGDP
成長率予測値は今後上方修正されるかもしれない。 
次回の「みずほGDPナウ」については、8月分の鉱工業生産や消費活動指数の結果等を踏まえて7～
9月期GDPのリアルタイム予測に取り組むこととし、10月中旬頃のレポート発刊を予定している。中東
情勢悪化やAI・データセンター投資増の影響が入り組む中、ナウキャストによる景気動向の把握はよ
り重要なものとなるだろう。

［参考文献］ 
浦沢聡士 （2023）「GDPナウキャストと景気判断～景気判断実務におけるGDPナウキャストの活用に向
けて～」 、内閣府経済社会総合研究所「経済分析」第208号 
太田晴康・仲山泰弘・酒井才介・松浦大将・越山祐資・西野洋平（2024） 「 「みずほGDPナウ」の推計
～DFMを用いた日本のGDPナウキャスティング～」 、 みずほリサーチ＆テクノロジーズ 『みずほ
インサイト』 、2024年8月30日 
酒井才介・西野洋平・太田晴康・仲山泰弘（2024）「「みずほ GDP ナウ」で見る景気動向～9 月中旬
時点で 7～9 月期 GDP は前期比＋0.0％と推計～」、みずほリサーチ＆テクノロジーズ
『Mizuho RT EXPRESS』、2024 年 9 月 19 日 
服部直樹（2026）「年率＋1.4％と 1 次速報から小幅に上方修正（4～6 月期 2 次 QE）」、みずほ総合

ーマンス評価等を踏まえ、採択するデータについては見直しを行う可能性がある。 
2 太田他（2024）で示した枠組みと同様、図表 2 の折れ線が各時点における実質GDP 成長率の予測値であり、月次の経済指標が
新たに公表されたり改定されたりすることで予測値が更新される。棒グラフは、予測値の改定幅、すなわち前回予測との差を各
月次経済指標で寄与度分解したもので、寄与度を合計するとモデル予測値の改定幅と一致する。なお、 「みずほGDP ナウ」は各
月次経済指標についても公表前に予測値を計算しており、各指標の寄与度はモデル予測値と実績値の乖離から計算されることか
ら、寄与度の符号は経済指標の変化の方向とは必ずしも一致しない。

## Document 013: 2026-09-25_［みずほ経済インサイト］AfD躍進で動揺する「防火壁」_e4ebf93d

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026-09-25_［みずほ経済インサイト］AfD躍進で動揺する「防火壁」_e4ebf93d.pdf
Report source: Mizuho
Date: 2026-09-25
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

AfD 躍進で動揺する「防火壁」 
今後各種政策へのAfDの影響が強まる可能性

○ ドイツで行われた州議会選挙では与党が勢力を後退させた一方、AfDが大きく躍進した。近年の
景気低迷や旧東西ドイツ間の差異が同党の支持拡大要因になっていると推測される 
○ 最大野党として勢いを増すAfDは政権の政策運営にも影響を及ぼし得る。また、同党の勢力拡大
が、主要政党が極右との連携を拒む「防火壁」の維持を難しくしていることも争点になっている 
○ AfDは今後も党勢拡大が予想され、将来的には国政でも「防火壁」の維持が困難となり政権入り
の可能性もある。同党の影響力拡大はドイツのみならず欧州全体の政治・経済に影響するだろう

１．党勢拡大が続くドイツのための選択肢 
ドイツでは、9月にザクセン＝アンハルト州、 メクレンブルク＝フォアポンメルン州、 ベルリンで州
議会選挙が実施された。メルツ首相率いるキリスト教民主同盟（CDU）は、メクレンブルク＝フォア
ポンメルン州で議席獲得に必要な得票率5％を下回り、 結党以来、 州レベルで初めて議席がゼロとなる
など、惨敗を喫した（図表1）。選挙結果を受けて、メルツ氏の辞任を求める声も上がるなど、従来か
ら低迷していた同氏への支持と求心力は、一段と低下している。一方で、大きく躍進したのが極右政
党であるドイツのための選択肢（AfD）1だ。特に、ザクセン＝アンハルト州でAfDが単独過半数に迫
図表 1 州・都市州議会の政党別議席占有率 図表2 連邦議会選挙と欧州議会選挙における

る議席を獲得したことは、これまでAfDとの連携を拒否してきた主要政党の政治戦略の持続可能性に
関する議論を巻き起こしている。 
このところAfDは、州議会選挙に加え、2024年の欧州議会選挙や昨年の連邦議会選挙でも躍進し、
国政で野党第一党となるなど党勢の拡大を続けている（図表2）。本稿では、AfDの支持拡大の背景を
分析したうえで、今後のドイツ並びに欧州政治・経済への影響を考察する。 
２．旧東西ドイツ間の差異や近年の景気低迷が AfD の支持拡大要因に 
AfDが支持される背景には、旧東西ドイツ間に未だ存在する差異や近年の景気低迷があるとみられ
る。

### Page 3

2021）および川畑（2024）を参考に、ロジスティック回帰による2つのモデルを用いてAfD支持者の属
性を分析した。結果は図表3の通りだ（使用データや推計方法、結果の解釈については補論を参照）。 
まず、モデル1では説明変数のうち、性別、年齢、学生、自国民主政治満足度、欧州統合支持、移民
が自国文化に与える影響がAfD支持と統計的に有意な関係を持つことが確認された。川 畑（2024）は 、
モデル1で統計的に有意な結果が得られた変数のうち、自国民主政治満足度、 欧州統合支持、 移民が自
国文化に与える影響がAfD支持率に影響を及ぼす背景について、旧東西ドイツ間の差異が存在する可
能性を指摘している。図表2・4の通り、AfDは旧東ドイツに属していた新連邦州2で特に支持される傾
向にあることで知られる3。そこで、こうした地域性を踏まえて、モデル1で有意な結果が得られた変
数のうち旧東ドイツ地域の十分なサンプル数を確保できるものに旧東ドイツ地域の回答者を1 とする
ダミー変数を乗じた交差項を追加したモデル2を推計した。その結果、自国民主政治満足度、EU統合
支持、移民が自国文化に与える影響について有意な逆相関関係が確認された。これらの変数は、数値
が上昇するほど肯定的な回答である。つまり、特に旧東ドイツ州で自国民主政治への不満や欧州統合
への否定的態度、移民が自国文化に悪影響を及ぼすという懸念がAfD支持と強く結びついていると解
釈できる。 
ドイツは統一から35年が経過してもなお旧東西間の格差が残存している。例えば、一人当たりGDP
の水準は旧東ドイツ州の方が大幅に低いほか、若年層を中心に経済的に豊かな西部への流出が続いて
いることで、 西ドイツ地域と比較して人口減少が急速に進んでいる （図表5）。こうした構造的な問題
に加えて、ここ数年の経済状況も東部におけるAfDの支持拡大を後押ししているとみられる。東西の
実質GDPの推移を比較すると、ウクライナ侵攻後、旧東ドイツ地域の方が落ち込みが大きく、両地域
の差が拡大している（図表6） 。 エネルギーコスト高や対中競争激化等による製造業の低迷に加えて旧
西ドイツ地域対比でウェイトが大きい建設業が高金利や資材価格上昇により低迷していることが背景
とみられる。 
加えて、東西統一が経済や政治面で安定・発展していた旧西ドイツに旧東ドイツが編入される形で

### Page 4

4

行われたことも、旧東ドイツ地域でのAfDの支持拡大につながったとみられる。旧西ドイツ主導の国
家体制整備が行われる過程で、東ドイツ地域の様々な分野の要職に西ドイツのエリート層が流入する
「エリート移転」が生じた。これが、東部出身者が経験や人的ネットワーク、昇進機会を得る余地を
狭め、 その後の持続的なキャリアアップの阻害要因になったと言われている4。実際、1990～2020年の
パネルデータを用いて東部出身者の管理職・上級管理職への到達機会を分析したHartmann（2024）に
よれば、東部地域の上級管理職において東部出身者は西部出身者より不利な地位に置かれているとし
ている。この差は学歴、職業経験、産業構造、社会的出身、失業経験等では十分に説明できず、統一
後の「エリート移転」の影響が持続している可能性を指摘している。また、Vogel et al.（2026）は、旧
東ドイツ地域出身者はドイツ人口の約19％を占めるにもかかわらず、同国の様々な分野の指導的ポス
トに占める東部出身者の割合は2024年時点で12.1％にとどまるとしており、依然として顕著な過少代
表が続いていることがうかがえる。 
こうした状況が自国民主政治への不満や政治・経済エリートへの反発につながり、「腐敗したエリ
ート対善良な市民」の構図で既存政府を批判するポピュリズムが拡大する要因になっていると考えら
れる。 現に、infratest dimapがザクセン＝アンハルト州で選挙当日に行った調査では、7割強の回答者が
「政治経済が旧西ドイツの人々に強く支配されている」、「旧東ドイツ地域住民は今も二等市民扱い
されている」との認識を示している。さらに、同調査では、旧東ドイツ地域の利益を最も代表できる
政党としてAfDを挙げた回答者の割合が最も多かった（図表7）。こうした結果は、東部住民の間に残
る代表性への不満や既成政治への反発が、AfDへの支持につながる土壌の一つになっていることを示
唆している。 
このような東部出身者の過少代表や二等市民意識は、主に東西統合を主導した旧西ドイツ出身の政
治エリートによって進められてきたEU統合等の政策への不信や不満を強めていると推測される。AfD
はこうした既成政治を批判しており、その訴えが旧東ドイツ地域での支持拡大を促す一因となってい
図表 6 ドイツ：地域別の実質 GDP 図表 7 旧東ドイツ地域の利益を

### Page 5

5

ると考えられる。 
また、旧東ドイツ地域は歴史的背景から移民が少なく文化的均質性が高いと言われている。このよ
うな社会構造が移民による自国文化への影響に対する懸念と移民受け入れに否定的なAfDの支持に強
く結びついていると考えられる5。  
３．今後、各種政策への AfD の影響が強まる可能性 
以上の複合的な要因からAfDは支持を拡大しているが、こうした傾向は今後も続くと予想される。 
旧東西ドイツ間の政治・経済的格差は短期的に解消可能なものではない。また、財政拡張によるイ
ンフラや防衛向けの投資増加を背景に景気は緩やかに持ち直しているものの、エネルギー価格の高止
まりや中国との競争激化等を背景に、これまでドイツ経済を支えてきた自動車や化学については回復
が見通せない。 加えて、 中東情勢緊迫化や猛暑の影響6によりインフレが押し上げられている。 Federle 
et al. （2024）は、予想外の物価上振れや景気下振れは急進的な政党の得票率を有意に押し上げると分
析しており、足元の物価上昇はAfDの追い風になり得る（図表8）。これらを踏まえれば、有権者の不
満は残存し、AfDが支持を得やすい状況は続くとみられる。 
また、AfDは旧東ドイツ地域を地盤としながら、特に男性を中心に若年・中年層では居住地域に関
わらず支持を広げている。 時系列データを用いているわけではないが、 前述のモデル2において年齢と
旧東ドイツダミーの交差項が有意な結果とならなかったことは、こうした状況を示唆している。同党
はSNSを通じた情報発信により、極右という印象を薄め、親しみやすい政党として浸透しているとみ
られる。今後、世代交代が進むことでAfDの支持は旧西ドイツ地域も含めて全国的に拡大することも
想定される。 
AfDは現時点では政権に参加していないものの、支持拡大により最大野党として無視できない勢力
となっている。 こうした状況を受けて、 与党はAfD
への支持者流出を抑えるため、 その政策や主張を
一定程度取り込む必要に迫られるだろう。AfDは、
減税や規制緩和・行政手続き簡素化、 エネルギー
コスト削減等に積極的であり、 これらの実現に向
けて政権に要求を強めることが想定される。 こう
した政策は、 家計の可処分所得や企業の事業環境
を改善するほか、 エネルギー集約型産業のコスト
負担を軽減することで景気にはプラスの影響を
与えるだろう。 
一方で、AfDが移民問題を争点化し支持を拡大
していることを踏まえれば、 移民政策はより厳格
化され得る。 実際、ショルツ前政権やメルツ政権
はAfDの支持拡大に押される形で、既に国境管理
や移民抑制策を導入している。AfDの勢力拡大は、
こうした政策の強化を一層促す要因となるだろ
う。 移民受入れの厳格化は人手不足を加速させる
図表 8 景気・物価の予想外の変動が

### Page 6

6

ほか、外国人材や海外企業にとってのドイツでの就労や投資先としての魅力低下につながることもあ
る。また、AfDは、メルツ政権が年金財政の安定化を目的として検討している支給開始年齢引き上げ
を含む年金改革や、労働時間規制の緩和に反対している。今後、国民の負担増加につながり得るこう
した政策の実現は一層難しくなるだろう。 
EUの政策にも影響が出る可能性がある。 ドイツは、 欧州委員会が提示した2028～34年の次期EU中期
予算（MFF）案について、既に数千億ユーロ規模の削減を求め、EUの追加的な共同借入にも反対して
いる。EUに懐疑的なAfDの台頭は、 ドイツのEU予算の削減を求める立場を強め、 次期MFFの合意形成
を一段と難しくする恐れがある。 
AfDは、 民族的・文化的・宗教的属性に基づく国民観、 党関係者によるナチスを巡る歴史認識に関す
る言動などが問題視されており、ザクセン＝アンハルト州の憲法擁護庁は同州のAfD支部を「確定的
な極右」 と分類している7。 ナチ党の政権獲得とその後の独裁化という歴史的経験を踏まえて、 主要政
党はこうした極右政党の政権参加や法案成立過程での連携を拒否する「防火壁（Brandmauer）」と呼
ばれる方針を維持してきた。しかしながら、今回の州議会選挙では、一部地域で「防火壁」を維持し
AfDを除く政権を組成することが難しくなっている現実を突きつける結果となった。 AfDの支持拡大
が今後も予想されることを踏まえれば、こうした展開は将来的に国政においても十分に想定される。 
今回の州議会選挙8のようにAfDが連邦議会でも過半数に迫る議席を得た場合、 主要政党が 「防火壁」
を維持するには、党派性の異なる複数政党による連立が必要となる。このような状況では連立交渉の
難航・長期化は避けられず、その間政治・政策運営は停滞することになろう。また、政権樹立に成功
しても、連立内で方針がまとまらず政権基盤が不安定化することも考えられる。こうした政治の停滞
や不安定化は政策不確実性を高め、企業の設備投資などを抑制する要因となり得る。 
また、そもそも 「防火壁」を維持しない、 あるいは （AfDが過半数議席を獲得することで） 維持でき
なくなり同党が政権に参加するケースも将来的には想定される9。AfDが政策に直接的に関与すること
になれば、野党として他党の政策修正を促す場合と比べて前述の規制緩和や減税、エネルギー政策見
直し等が加速することで、低迷が続く一部製造業には追い風となるだろう。一方、財政や移民政策に
ついては懸念事項が残る。同党は財政規律を重視する姿勢を示しているものの、その公約は財源の裏
付けが乏しいと言われている10。財政への信認が損なわれることによる金利上昇や財源確保を目的と
する重要政策の予算削減とこれに伴う不確実性上昇のリスクが高まるだろう。移民の流入が大幅に減
少することで人手不足に拍車がかかり、インフレや景気下振れが深刻化するリスクも指摘されている
11。 
AfDはドイツのユーロ圏離脱とマルクの再導入や EUを主権国家間の緩やかな協力枠組みに再編す
ることを主張するなど、 国家主権重視の立場からEUに極めて懐疑的だ。 こうしたスタンスを踏まえれ
ば、 同党が政権に関与する場合、EUの重要政策目標の一つである市場の統合やEUレベルで進む防衛力
強化等の停滞が懸念される12。加えて、親ロシア的な立場をとり、 ロシアとの接触がたびたび問題にな
ってきたAfDが政権に加われば、対ロシア制裁やウクライナ支援に関するEU内合意形成が難しくなる
ほか、安全保障上の懸念を抱く他の欧州諸国やEUとの外交関係悪化も予想される。 
第二次世界大戦後、極右勢力の政権参加を抑制する政治的・制度的枠組みを維持してきたが、AfD
の勢力拡大はこうした枠組みに強い圧力をかけており、ドイツ政治は大きな曲がり角を迎えている。
AfDの支持拡大は、 ドイツのみならず欧州全体の政治・経済に影響を及ぼし得る。 それだけに、 州議会

### Page 7

7

選挙後の政局がどのように展開するのか、AfDがメルツ政権下の連邦政治でどこまで影響力を拡大す
るのか、そして与党をはじめとする主要政党が「防火壁」を維持しつつ有権者の不満にいかに応える
のかは、重要な焦点となろう。

補論 
（１） 推計に用いたデータ 
政治・社会・経済に関する態度および個人の属性について国際比較可能な個票データを提供する欧
州社会調査 （ESS）が 、2023年から2024年にかけて実施した調査のデータを使用した。なお、今回の分
析は特定の支持政党を持つ回答者のデータのみを用いている。 
（２） 推計手法と結果の解釈 
被説明変数にAfDを支持する者を1それ以外を0とする二値変数を、説明変数には回答者の基本的な
社会経済属性を規定する年齢 （実数） 、 性別 （男性＝1）、所 得（1～10の値をとり、10が最も高所得） 、
教育水準 （1～8の値をとり、8が最も高学歴） 、 職業状況 （就業者、 失業者、 主夫・主婦、 年金生活者、
障がい・疾病のそれぞれを1とする二値変数）に加えて、自己生活満足度（0～10の値をとり、10が最
も満足度が高い）、自国経済満足度（0～10の値をとり、10が最も満足度が高い）、自国民主政治満足
度（0～10の値をとり、10が最も満足度が高い）、欧州統合支持（0～10の値をとり、10が最も統合を
支持）、伝統重視（1～6の値をとり、6が最も伝統重視）、規範重視（1～6の値をとり、6が最も規範
重視）、愛国心（0～10の値をとり、10が最も愛国心が強い）、自由重視（1～6の値をとり、6が最も
自由重視）、移民が自国経済に与える影響（0～10の値をとり、10が最も肯定的）、移民が自国文化に
与える影響 （0～10の値をとり、10が最も肯定的） などの回答者の政治志向や移民への態度を表す変数
を採用し、ロジスティック回帰を行った。また、モデル2には旧東ドイツ地域に居住する回答者を1と
するダミー変数を追加している。なお、二値変数以外の説明変数の尺度は一様ではなく、係数の大小
比較に適さないため、標準化したうえで推計を行っている。 
図表3にまとめた結果の解釈については次の通りだ。モデル1の自国民主政治満足度を例にとると、
同変数が1標準偏差上昇するとAfDを支持するオッズ （＝AfD支持の起こりやすさ）は26％に低下する。
すなわち、自国の民主政治に対する満足度が高い人ほどAfDを支持しにくく、逆に民主政治への不満
が強い人ほどAfDを支持する傾向があることを示している。また、二値変数については、例えばモデ
ル1の性別であれば男性のAfDを支持するオッズは女性の235％（2.35倍）となることを意味する。

1 もともと AfD は、欧州債務危機の際にメルケル政権による南欧諸国への救済措置に不満を抱く経済学者らを中心に結成され、
「教授政党」とも呼ばれていた。当初は EU やユーロ圏からの離脱など、主に経済・通貨政策を掲げており、必ずしも現在のよ
うな排外主義的な政党ではなかったと言われている。しかし、その後は党内で右派的勢力が強くなったことに加え、シリアなど
から多くの難民が流入した 2015 年の欧州難民危機と、当時のメルケル政権による移民・難民政策への世論の反発を背景に排外
主義的な主張を前面に押し出す政党へと変質していった 
2 メクレンブルク＝フォアポンメルン州、ブランデンブルク州、ザクセン＝アンハルト州、ザクセン州、テューリンゲン州

はいずれも新連邦州だ

5 Arzheimer（2017）は、旧東ドイツ地域では戦後から東西統一前までドイツ社会主義統一党（SED）が支配的地位を占め、政党
間競争や自由選挙を経験してこなかったため、西ドイツのような特定政党への長期的な帰属意識が形成されにくかったと指摘し
ている。そのため、統一後に CDU や SPD などの西ドイツの政党が活動を拡大しても強固で安定した支持基盤を築くことは容易
ではなく、AfD のような新興政党が支持を獲得しやすい条件の一つになったとみられる 
6 猛暑が欧州の経済・物価に与える影響については川畑・北川（2026）を参照 
7 また、全国的にも、連邦憲法擁護庁は 2025 年に AfD 全体を「確定的な極右」と位置付けた（現在は司法判断が出るまで保留
されている） 
8 ドイツでは、州政府の構成員が連邦参議院（上院）において各州を代表する仕組みとなっている。各州には人口に応じて 3～6
票が配分され、法案等の審議・議決を通じて州の利害を連邦政治に反映する。今回の州議会選挙後に AfD が州政府に参画すれ
ば、連邦参議院における審議や投票を通じて連邦政治にも直接的に影響を及ぼすことになる。なお、各州の票は分割せず一括し
て投じなければならず、州政府を構成する政党間で投票方針を統一できない場合、当該州は事実上棄権となる 
9 「防火壁」を維持することで、AfD に「主要政党が民意を軽視している」という批判材料を与え、かえって同党を勢いづかせ
ているとも言われている

11 ザクセン＝アンハルト州を分析対象とした Gropp and Reifschneider（2026）は、AfD 主導の州政権成立に伴って排外的な政治
風土が強まり、移民の流入が減少すれば、2027～31 年の 5 年間で約 1 万 4,000 人の追加的な人手不足が生じ、18～32 億ユーロ
（同州 GDP の約 2～4％）の付加価値が失われると試算している。なお、Dorsch et al.（2026）についても言えることだが、州支
部と連邦レベルではスタンスの相違点も多いため単純化はできない点には留意が必要だ

## Document 157: AI“砸饭碗”担忧暂缓！埃森哲845亿年订单创纪录、指引超预期，股价盘中暴涨超20%｜财报见闻

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI“砸饭碗”担忧暂缓！埃森哲845亿年订单创纪录、指引超预期，股价盘中暴涨超20%｜财报见闻.pdf

### Page 1

埃森哲第四财季业绩全线超预期，营收同⽐增 6% 、超过公司指引上限，较分析师预期⾼
3.5% ，新订单同⽐增 4% ⾄近 220 亿美元、较分析师预期⾼近 11% ；本财年营收指引最⾼增
6% 、均值 4.5% ，⾼于市场预期增幅 3.9% 。 AI 需求成为订单新推⼿， CEO 称客⼾在公司帮
助下建设数据基础设施和 AI 能⼒，以便更⼤规模应⽤ AI 。埃森哲收涨 16% ，带动 IT 咨询业
股整体⾛⾼。
摘要：
在交出超预期的季度业绩、创纪录订单以及⾼于市场预期的全年营收指引后，全球咨询与 IT 服务
巨头埃森哲股价暴涨。
美东时间 10 ⽉ 1 ⽇周四，埃森哲（ ACN ）股价跳空⾼开近 18% ，盘中涨幅曾超过 20% 、达到
24% ，收涨近 16% ，创将近七个⽉来收盘新⾼。埃森哲⼤涨不仅带动 IBM 、 Wipro 、 Infosys 、
Cognizant 等 IT 咨询和服务业公司股价⾛⾼，也成为近期市场重新审视 “AI 是否会削弱传统咨询需
求 ” 的⼀个重要信号。

埃森哲财报显⽰，截⾄ 8 ⽉ 31 ⽇的公司 2026 财年第四财季，营收同⽐增⻓ 6% ⾄ 186.8 亿美元，⾼
于公司⾃⾝指引上限，较市场预期⾼逾 3.5% 。当季调整后每股收益（ EPS ）为 3.29 美元，较分
析师预期⾼逾 3.1% 。
更受市场关注的是订单。埃森哲第四季度新订单达到 221.7 亿美元，较分析师预期订单额⾼近
11% ，订单收⼊⽐（ book-to-bill ）为 1.2 ； 2026 财年全年新订单达到创纪录的 845 亿美元。公司
当季还录得 141 笔⾦额⾄少 1 亿美元的客⼾订单，创季度新⾼。
本次埃森哲财报尤其受到市场关注，是因为过去⼀年投资者⼀直担⼼，⽣成式 AI 和智能体
（ Agent ）可能让企业减少对传统 IT 咨询、软件开发和外包服务的需求，从⽽冲击埃森哲的核⼼
业务。此次创纪录的订单以及并未明显放缓的业绩指引，则暂时缓解了这种担忧。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

AI 焦虑曾重压股价  ⽽企业 AI 需求成为订单新推⼿
过去⼀年，随着⽣成式 AI 和智能体加速进⼊企业软件开发、数据分析和业务流程，市场⼀直担⼼
企业借助 AI 减少对传统咨询、 IT 外包以及⼤量⼈⼯服务的需求，进⽽冲击埃森哲的核⼼业务。
这⼀担忧今年⼀度明显拖累埃森哲股价。截⾄周三收盘，埃森哲股价今年九个⽉内累计跌幅超
过 30% 。但周四公布的财报给出了截然不同的信号：企业客⼾对咨询和 IT 服务的需求并未因 AI 快
速发展⽽明显萎缩。
埃森哲第四财季新订单同⽐增⻓ 4% ⾄ 221.7 亿美元，远超分析师约 200 亿美元的预期订单额；
2026 财年全年新订单达 845 亿美元，创财年最⾼纪录。公司当季还录得 141 笔⾦额⾄少 1 亿美元
的客⼾订单，同样创下季度新⾼。
其中，第四财季咨询业务新订单为 94 亿美元，托管服务新订单达到 127.7 亿美元，后者的订单收
⼊⽐达到 1.4 。
媒体指出，强劲的订单和营收表现缓解了投资者对 AI 可能 “ 挤压 ” 传统咨询需求的担忧。

与此同时，埃森哲表⽰，固定价格项⽬⽬前已经占到订单总额的 65% 以上，其中包括以成果为
导向的项⽬。
埃森哲 CEO Julie Sweet 表⽰，客⼾正在利⽤埃森哲帮助其建设数据基础设施和 AI 能⼒，以便在
更⼤规模上应⽤ AI 。她还提到， FedEx 、 BP 等⼤型客⼾正在进⼀步扩⼤ AI 在各⾃业务中的应
⽤。
这意味着，⾄少从当前订单情况来看， AI 并未简单地转化为传统咨询需求的替代品，反⽽正在催
⽣企业建设数据基础设施、 AI 技术栈以及相关应⽤的新需求。
各地区、各⾏业业务均增⻓，第四财季营收超公司指引上限
从刚刚过去的⼀个季度看，埃森哲的增⻓并⾮来⾃单⼀业务。
第四财季埃森哲营收为 186.8 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 6% ，按当地货币计算增⻓ 7% ，不仅超过市场预
期 180.4 亿美元，也⾼于公司此前给出的整个指引区间 177.5 亿⾄ 184 亿美元。
其中，咨询业务营收 92.8 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 6% ；托管服务营收 94 亿美元，同⽐增⻓ 7% 。第四
季度 GAAP ⼝径下营业利润率达到 15.3% ，同⽐提⾼ 370 个基点。全年营收则达到 742 亿美元，

同⽐增⻓ 6% 。
评论称，埃森哲在所有地区市场以及各⾏业客⼾中均实现营收增⻓，其中通信、媒体和科技⾏
业增⻓尤其强劲。
全财年调整后 EPS 为 13.97 美元，同⽐增⻓ 8% ；⾃由现⾦流达到 116 亿美元，并向股东返还 115
亿美元，其中股票回购及赎回⾦额达到 75 亿美元。

### Page 3

本财年营收预计增⻓ 3% ⾄ 6% ， EPS 指引覆盖市场预期
更值得市场关注的是埃森哲对 2027 财年的展望。
埃森哲预计， 2027 财年按当地货币计算的营收将增⻓ 3% ⾄ 6% ，⾼于市场约 3.9% 的平均预期；
全年 GAAP 摊薄后 EPS 预计为 14.39 ⾄ 14.81 美元。公司还预计全年营业利润率达到 15.9% ⾄
16.1% ，较 2026 财年 GAAP 营业利润率扩⼤ 50 ⾄ 70 个基点。
分析师预计埃森哲 2027 财年营收约为 764 亿美元、 EPS 为 14.64 美元。埃森哲的 EPS 指引区间覆
盖了市场预期，区间均值 14.6 美元⼤致符合预期，⽽营收增速指引的上限 6% 明显⾼于分析师的
增⻓预期。
相⽐之下，公司对当前 2027 财年第⼀季度的指引更为具体：预计营收为 189.5 亿⾄ 196 亿美元，
⽽分析师此前预计为 193.5 亿美元。这意味着公司的季度营收指引区间同样覆盖市场预期。
从盈利能⼒看，埃森哲预计 2027 财年营业利润率达到 15.9% ⾄ 16.1% ，较 2026 财年 GAAP 营业

利润率 15.4% 提⾼ 50 ⾄ 70 个基点；同时预计全年经营现⾦流为 119 亿⾄ 127 亿美元，并计划向股
东返还⾄少 95 亿美元现⾦。
⼈员增⻓模式或被 AI 重塑
不过， AI 对埃森哲的影响并⾮只有需求端。这份财报并不意味着 AI 对咨询⾏业的⻓期冲击已经消
失。
英国《⾦融时报》指出， AI 正在帮助埃森哲提升效率和⼈均收⼊，同时也可能意味着未来员⼯数
量的增⻓速度放缓。公司此前⼀年曾经历裁员，但近⼏个⽉⼜新增约 1.5 万名员⼯，⽬前员⼯总

节今年为 11 ⽉ 26 ⽇，如果 11 ⽉ 9 ⽇当周正式启动路演， Anthropic 将有机会赶在这⼀假期前完成

规模或⽐肩 SpaceX ，投资者给出近 2 万亿美元估值
Anthropic 此次上市的⼀⼤看点是发⾏规模。

道显⽰，部分潜在投资者认为， Anthropic 的合理估值可能达到 1.8 万亿美元⾄ 2 万亿美元。
这⼀估值将使 Anthropic 跻⾝全球市值最⼤的 AI 公司之列，也将成为资本市场衡量⽣成式 AI 公司
估值⽔平的重要参照。
与此同时，竞争对⼿ OpenAI 近期选择暂缓 IPO 。据此前报道， OpenAI 计划筹集⾄少 300 亿美元
新资⾦，⽬标估值约为 1.4 万亿美元。 OpenAI CEO Sam Altman 则表⽰，公司⽬前并不急于上
市。
如果 Anthropic 率先登陆公开市场，其发⾏定价也将为投资者重新评估整个 AI ⾏业的估值⽔平提

供⼀个重要样本。
企业客⼾撑起增⻓故事

企业⽤⼾中的采⽤率较⾼，能够为投资者看重的持续收⼊增⻓提供⽀撑。
包括银⾏、对冲基⾦以及 Salesforce 等企业软件公司在内的多类机构，都已经与 Anthropic 合
作，将其 AI ⼯具整合进⾃⾝⼯作流程。
这⼀企业市场布局，也构成 Anthropic 与 OpenAI 之间的重要差异之⼀。对于准备进⼊公开市场的
AI 公司⽽⾔，企业客⼾带来的持续使⽤和商业化收⼊，是投资者评估增⻓持续性的重要指标。

营收暴增逾 10 倍，但算⼒投⼊带来巨额亏损
Anthropic 的⾼估值建⽴在 AI 需求快速增⻓之上，但公司仍处于⼤规模投⼊阶段。
此前媒体获得的⽂件显⽰， Anthropic 2025 年全年营收约为 46 亿美元，远⾼于 2024 年的 3.86 亿
美元；但同期净亏损接近 420 亿美元，约为上⼀年的 5 倍。

与此同时， Anthropic 正在⼤规模扩充 AI 基础设施。近期公司已与 Akamai 签署价值约116 亿美
元、期限七年的计算资源协议，进⼀步扩⼤未来的算⼒投⼊。

这也意味着， Anthropic 需要向公开市场证明的并不只是 AI 业务的⾼速增⻓，还包括在持续增加
算⼒和基础设施投⼊的情况下，未来如何将收⼊增⻓转化为更稳定的现⾦流和盈利能⼒。
OpenAI 追赶， AI 安全争议也在升温
Anthropic 过去⼀段时间的快速崛起，也⾯临竞争格局变化。
媒体指出， OpenAI 近⼏个⽉销售势头增强，正在重新加⼤对 Anthropic 的竞争压⼒。两家公司同
时处于 AI 安全讨论的中⼼，近期⼀系列⾼调的 AI 智能体⿊客事件进⼀步引发外界对前沿模型安全
⻛险的关注。
Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei 9 ⽉还在个⼈⽹站发表⽂章，主张放缓前沿 AI 模型能⼒的推进速
度。

值可以达到 1.8 万亿⾄ 2 万亿美元。
AI 巨星上市正逢美国 IPO 市场承压

股票的加权平均回报率约为 -4% ，明显落后于同期上涨约 12% 的标普 500 指数和上涨约 20% 的纳
斯达克 100 指数。
近期美国 IPO 市场也连续出现推迟上市的案例，部分公司甚⾄在原定发⾏前临时叫停。在这⼀背
景下， Anthropic 如果最终完成超⼤规模 IPO ，将成为今年美国新股市场最受关注的交易之⼀。
其上市定价能否获得公开市场认可，也将成为观察 AI 超级公司⾼估值能否从私募市场向公开市场
传导的重要窗⼝。

## Document 187: Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-28).pdf
Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.
S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index is an early monthly economic indicator that estimates overall private-sector business activity by combining manufacturing

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

## Document 238: Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital

### Page 1

1. There are two themes dominating our investor conversations: the impact of AI and 
the rise in interest rates. The two issues are linked as demand for capital from both 
the private and public sectors increase. In the private sector a surge in capex 
spending to fund AI infrastructure has eaten into free cash flow and forced 
companies to raise more in debt and equity markets. Meanwhile, government 
borrowing needs have increased as priorities shift towards upgrading critical 
infrastructure, energy security and defense at a time when cyclical inflationary 
pressures driven by higher energy prices are also resulting in higher policy rates. The 
combination has pushed up the cost of capital. As recently as 2022, for example, 
30-year bond yields in Germany and Japan were close to zero (Exhibit 1). A rise in 
yields, together with more uncertainty (over geopolitics and the future impact of AI) 
have, in combination, pushed up the cost of capital.

2. This rise in yields comes after a near-record period of equity outperformance 
relative to bonds over 10-year holding periods (Exhibit 2).

issue that has caused the biggest impact in the recent past. As our US strategists note, 
stocks have usually generated positive returns alongside rising interest rates unless the 
pace of rising rates exceeded 2+ standard deviations and this is a pattern that is broadly

reflected in other regions too. Today, a 2-standard deviation move in 10-year Treasury

the adjustments in recent days which explains the pullback in equity prices.

4. But despite the rise in yields, nominal GDP and profit growth remain strong and, until 
recently, the impact of rising bond yields has been offset by the strength in corporate 
profits. Earnings growth has been the main driver of equity returns over the past 18 
months in all regions (Exhibit 5). This strength has meant that PE multiples have either 
been flat (Japan and Europe) or have fallen (the US, Asia and EM).

In the US, in particular, the forward PE multiple for the S&P has come down from 22x at

the start of the year to 19x, in line with its long-run average, despite the market being

5. While US equities have driven the bulk of equity outperformance for much of the 
fifteen years post the financial crisis, the improvement in earnings across regions, and 
the sharper de-rating in the US, has resulted in equity performance being 
broader-based by geography. Since 2025, the US equity market has been the weakest of 
the major regions (Exhibit 6), a turnaround from the dominant trend since the financial 
crisis and increasing investor returns from a more geographically diversified portfolio.

6. Supportive profit growth has been accompanied by positive earnings revisions, with 
2026 and 2027 estimates moving higher across major regions. There are four broad 
areas which have been driving much of this profit growth. First, technology earnings 
continue to be very robust. Second, rising energy prices have pushed up profits in the 
commodity sector. Third, banks have generally enjoyed strong earnings backed by 
positive nominal GDP growth, steep yield curves and strong private sector balance 
sheets. Finally, sectors such as Industrials have benefited from the surge in AI capex 
spending which has spilled over into improved revenues for the ‘pick and shovels’ of AI 
infrastructure. The breadth of the earnings’ growth has also increased the opportunity 
for investors to diversify across sectors as well as countries.

7. While technology profit growth has remained strong, the surge in capex spending 
among the hyperscalers has increasingly eaten through their free cash flow (Exhibit 8) 
prompting companies to look for alternative sources of funding, in the credit and equity 
markets.  The capex spending growth for AA-rated issuers has been substantial: the 65% 
year-over-year growth in Q2 marks the 10th consecutive quarter that aggregate AA 
capex growth exceeds 35%. They have also turned to the convertible bond market 
where volume has also increased year to date reaching $135 billion in the US, with 
AI-related borrowers driving 44% of total issuance, on our estimates. 
Our Credit team raised their full-year US IG gross issuance forecast by $200bn to $2.3 
trillion and AI-related issuers account for a quarter of USD IG gross supply this year, 
pushing overall supply to a year-to-date record.

### Page 7

surge. Globally technology has seen its PE ratio moderate, falling below its 20-year 
median (Exhibit 10). Meanwhile, the spending of these companies has spilled over into 
better earnings and more visible cash flows in many other parts of the economy. 
Healthcare, Consumer Discretionary and Industrials are all more expensive in absolute 
terms than IT, and Industrials has re-rated and now has a PE close to the 90th percentile 
of its 20-year range.

9. For these reasons, we have argued that Technology equities have not experienced a 
classic bubble because valuation levels are much lower than in previous financial 
bubbles. Nevertheless, the risk for investors is less about valuation and more whether 
there is an unsustainable earnings bubble. Bubbles in earnings, rather than valuations, 
have occurred in previous periods.  The Bank sector, for example, briefly became the 
biggest sector in the S&P 500 in the run up to the financial crisis of 2008/09 (Exhibit 11). 
Unlike the technology bubble of the late 1990s, or the Japanese bubble of the late 
1980s, Bank stocks did not experience a major valuation bubble at the time, but the 
surge in earnings that drove the sector’s outperformance proved to be unsustainable.

### Page 8

There are, however, important differences to the Tech sector today. The earnings of the 
banks were boosted by sharply rising leverage and the asset that they were financing – 
US real estate – did experience a valuation bubble. When the housing market collapsed, 
pushing economies into recession, it triggered a sharp fall in underlying earnings for the 
banking sector. There are important differences today. First, technology profits remain 
very robust, and balance sheets are strong overall. Second, interest coverage ratios for 
the aggregate S&P 500 and its median stock, for example, rank in the 99th and 68th 
percentiles relative to the past 20 years. Third, demand for compute is accelerating and 
outstripping supply; Microsoft recently declared their intent to triple data centre 
capacity in 6 years. The bottlenecks are increasingly power, land and data centre 
capacity rather than a lack of compute demand. At our annual Communacopia 
Technology Conference, Nvidia reiterated its 2030 AI TAM outlook to $3tn-$4tn and 
supply constraints continue to be broad based. Nonetheless, any slowdown in profit 
growth, in an environment of a much higher cost of capital, could put downward 
pressure on equity prices, reducing confidence in future cash flows across the ecosystem 
from the hyperscalers to the ‘pick and shovels’ that have been benefiting from the capex 
boom.  
10. Given the near-term risks in bond markets, we remain neutral on equities, alongside 
other asset classes on a 3-month horizon, but continue to be overweight equities over 
12 months. While profit growth is likely to slow from the recent strong pace, ongoing 
growth in nominal GDP should underpin equity markets and the earnings cycle 
propelling equity returns even without any valuation expansion from current levels and 
our economists continue to see a low risk of recession. As our asset allocation team has 
shown, earnings growth and ROE are likely to continue to be the key drivers of equity 
performance relative to bonds, and we expect these fundamentals to remain supportive 
for equity returns. Nevertheless, we continue to recommend well diversified equity 
exposure as opportunities continue to broaden across geographies, sectors and factors.

## Document 271: Optimistic Spin_ Bond Market Pricing Better-Than-Expected Economic Growth

Source PDF: all report pdf/Optimistic Spin_ Bond Market Pricing Better-Than-Expected Economic Growth.pdf
Report source: Substack Reports
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Optimistic Spin: Bond Market Pricing Better-Than-Expected Economic Growth
Date:    3 hours ago
Optimistic Spin: Bond Market Pricing Better-Than-Expected Economic Growth

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Optimistic Spin: Bond Market
Pricing Better-Than-Expected
Economic Growth
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 30 Sept 2026
View in browser
Photo by Free Walking Tour Salzburg / Unsplash

Bond yields rose yet again today even though September's PCED inflation
report showed some improvement, which was due mostly to new
measurement procedures. Inflation remains about a percentage point
above the Fed's 2.0% target. Other economic indicators today confirmed
that the economy and the labor market are doing very well and
strengthened the case for more Fed rate hikes. Yesterday's dovish

### Page 2

suggestion by NY Fed President John Williams that a pause in rate hikes
might make sense was all but forgotten today.
The most optimistic explanation for the backup in bond yields is that it
reflects better-than-expected economic growth, suggesting the economy's
R-Star (i.e., the neutral interest rate) is higher than Fed officials thought at
the beginning of the year. Back then, they mostly agreed that the federal
funds rate was still slightly restrictive, i.e., above the neutral rate. Now,

they agree with Fed Chair Kevin Warsh that September's 25bps rate hike
"removed a dose of accommodation." That implies that the federal funds
rate is below neutral.
No wonder the 2-year Treasury yield remained 100bps above the federal
funds rate today (chart).
Federal funds futures are pricing in three to four 25bps rate hikes over the
next 12 months, including roughly two over the next six months (chart).

### Page 3

Let's review the latest data:
(1) Inflation. Today’s August PCED report confirmed that the strong US
economy still has an inflation problem. Headline PCED rose 0.3% m/m
and 3.4% y/y, while core PCED increased 0.2% m/m and 3.0% y/y. Both
remain well above the Fed’s 2% target.
The August PCED report incorporated the annual revisions and
methodological changes implemented by the Bureau of Economic
Analysis. July's headline PCED inflation rate was revised down 37bps, to

2.98% y/y from 3.35%, while core PCED was cut 34bps, to 3.36% from
3.70%. The key point is that much of the recent decline in core PCED
inflation reflects revised measurement procedures rather than a genuine
improvement in underlying inflation.

### Page 4

Indeed, despite the methodological changes, the report's details point to
sticky underlying inflation. Goods PCED inflation rose to 3.6% y/y in
August from 3.3% in July, partly reflecting a 4.1% m/m jump in gasoline
prices (chart). Tariffs and the AI buildout added further pressure: prices for
computers and peripherals surged 3.8% m/m, while toy prices rose 2.0%.
Looking ahead, tariff pass-through and AI-related demand should keep
goods inflation elevated, while the renewed rise in oil and refined-product

prices in September adds another source of upward pressure.
The PCED services inflation rate eased to 3.4% y/y in August (chart).
However, the “supercore” PCED services, which excludes both energy
and housing, rose 0.4% m/m in August. That was the strongest monthly
increase since May 2026 and pushed the y/y rate up to 3.5%.
The supercore inflation rate isolates some of the stickiest and most wage-
sensitive parts of the inflation basket. The measure remains well above a

pace consistent with the Fed’s 2% target. More importantly, it has been
moving higher since October 2025.

### Page 5

(2) Consumer spending and income. Consumer spending remains
remarkably strong. Inflation-adjusted consumption rose to another record
high in August (chart). By contrast, real disposable personal income was
unchanged m/m and has been essentially flat for more than a year. That
trend is likely to persist as more Baby Boomers retire, slowing wage
income growth.
Real consumer spending rose 0.6% m/m in August, the strongest monthly
gain since March 2025. The increase was broad-based, with especially

strong spending on discretionary categories including restaurants and
hotels, recreation, and apparel (chart).

### Page 6

The surge in household net worth relative to disposable income is causing
consumers, especially Baby Boomers, to reduce their savings rate (chart).
August’s saving rate was revised meaningfully higher, but still fell to 4.1%,
the lowest since November 2022. We expect it to decline further as more
Boomers retire. Their labor income drops to zero when they do so, but
their sizable accumulated wealth lets them keep spending.
(3) Labor market. Another key driver of consumer spending is the strong

labor market. ADP private payrolls rose by 90,000 in September, with
healthy gains across both goods-producing and service industries (chart).
We expect Friday’s employment report to confirm that strength, with
nonfarm payrolls likely to show a 100,000 increase in September.

### Page 7

(4) GDP. Q2's real GDP growth was revised today from 1.5% saar to
2.2%. On the other hand, Q3's growth rate was revised down from 5.0% to
3.7% by the Atlanta Fed's GDPNow tracking model (chart). That was
mostly attributable to the widening trade deficit. Final sales to private
domestic purchasers was strong with a 4.5% gain.
💡
Join the discussion with Dr. Ed below! Paid members can now
leave comments or questions at the end of our QuickTakes, and it

may be featured in the next "Just Ask Dr. Ed!"

### Page 8

## Document 282: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors

Source PDF: all report pdf/Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Thematic Reports)
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 18
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

WS Global CIO Office

InvesTips

Roots and routes –  
A framework for Global 
Indian investors

September 2026

### Page 2

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  2

India’s financial coming of age has created a generation of investors at a 
distinctive crossroads: deeply connected to one of the world’s fastest -growing 
economies, yet increasingly aware that portfolios concentrated in a single market 
can accumulate risks over time. 
This report discusses the challenges faced by two distinct ‘Global Indian’ investor 
profiles. The first is the onshore Indian investor: a resident Indian whose wealth 
is largely denominated in domestic equities and fixed income, but who may still treat 
global markets as an occasional satellite allocation rather than a core portfolio 
component. The second is the offshore Indian investor : a non -resident Indian 
(NRI) based outside India with full access to global capital markets, yet who may 
be under-allocated to the growth story unfolding in their country of origin. Because 
global benchmarks understate India’s economic importance, investors seeking 
more meaningful participation in its growth story may consider allocating above 
benchmark levels, with NRIs’ potential stronger  affinity with India providing an 
additional reason to make this adjustment. 
For both groups, the starting point is the same: a concentrated bias towards either 
Indian or global markets can create risks that diversification can help mitigate 
without requiring investors to abandon their core convictions. For onshore Indian 
investors, global assets can provide structural currency diversification and access 
to sectors that are under -represented in Indian indices. Our Capital Market 
Assumptions (CMAs)  illustrate the potential portfolio benefits: increasing  
the allocation to global assets from 0% to 15% raises expected returns while 
reducing expected volatility meaningfully. As a result, the portfolio’s risk -adjusted 
returns improve. 
For offshore Indian investors, India offers a real yield premium, a rising weight in global indices and long-term growth that remains 
meaningfully under -represented in global benchmarks. A historical perspective illustrates the opportunity: since 2001,  
Indian equities have delivered over 10% annualised returns in USD terms, outperforming Developed Market equities over the 
same period. 
Onshore and offshore Indian investors may share the same connection to India, but they face very different portfolio challenges. 
There are key dividing lines that determine how each investor should think about diversification, risk management and the rol e 
of Indian assets within a broader investment portfolio.

Manpreet Gill 
Chief Investment Officer, EMEA 
Sylvain Huard 
Head of Asset Allocation 
Hannah Chew 
Portfolio Strategist

1 Two investor profiles, one shared 
opportunity

### Page 3

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  3

Base currency –  
the first and most 
important divide 
 The onshore Indian investor earns, spends, saves and ultimately retires in rupees (INR). Their 
relevant return is, therefore, an INR return. If the INR depreciates against the US dollar (USD), 
foreign assets rise in INR terms and can act as a natural hedge . If the INR appreciates, those 
same assets deliver lower INR returns than their local -currency performance would suggest. 
For the offshore investor, the currency problem is reversed. Their benchmark is typically USD-
based, and Indian markets are the exposure that introduces exchange-rate risk. A strong year 
for Indian equities in INR terms may look less compelling once translated back into USD at an 
unfavourable exchange rate.

Regulatory access –  
different constraints, 
different routes 
 The onshore Indian investor operates within the Liberalised Remittance Scheme (LRS) 
framework. Depending on their circumstances, this can offer meaningful or constrained access 
to global markets. Fund -of-funds and international exchange -traded funds (ETFs)  can also 
offer alternative access routes, as opposed to direct securities alone.  
For the offshore Indian investor, the challenge is less about access to India than choosing the 
most appropriate investment route. They can generally invest through onshore accounts, while 
offshore-listed funds and ETFs may offer a more convenient alternat ive. The preferred 
structure will depend on their residency, tax domicile and product eligibility, making careful 
planning important even where access itself is relatively broad. 
As an example, India’s Gujarat International Finance Tec -City (GIFT City), home to the 
country’s International Financial Services Centre (IFSC), is one potential implementation route 
available to both onshore and offshore Indian investors. For offshore investors, GIFT City offers 
an efficient way to access India-linked and global investment products with little or no need for 
INR conversion, while allowing foreign -currency repatriation. For onshore investors, it can 
facilitate USD -denominated exposure throu gh foreign -currency accounts under the LRS, 
providing a domestically regulated, yet globally oriented platform for portfolio diversification.

Portfolio risk –  
concentration on 
one side, under-
allocation on the 
other 
 The onshore Indian investor’s core risk is concentration and complacency: a portfolio almost 
entirely exposed to India’s domestic economic cycle, policy environment and sectoral 
composition. This is not a prediction that these will deteriorate, as India’s structural growth story 
remains compelling. Rather, this implies the risk is structural when all equity exposure sits 
within a single market, causing the portfolio to react simultaneously to the same domestic 
catalysts. By contrast, the offshore Indian investor faces the opposite problem: under-allocation 
to one of the world’s fastest-growing economies, driven partly by access frictions and partly by 
the difficulty of sizing a home-market allocation through a global investment framework.

A diversified portfolio makes sense 
regardless of the investor profile 
Before examining the specific assets available to Global 
Indian investors, it is useful to step back and ask a more 
fundamental question: why hold multiple asset classes at all? 
The simple answer is diversification. Because different asset 
classes play dif ferent roles and perform differently across 
economic and market conditions, combining them thoughtfully 
can reduce risk, create more consistent outcomes and build 
greater portfolio resilience than relying on any single asset 
class alone. 
Each major asset class tends to perform best under different 
economic conditions. Equities usually benefit when corporate 
earnings are growing and investor confidence is strong, but 
they can fall sharply when growth disappoints or credit 
conditions tighten. Bonds can provide income and may hold 
their value, or even appreciate, when growth slows and 
central banks ease policy, but they typically face pressure 
when inflation is high and interest rates rise. Gold has long 
been viewed as a geopolitical and infla tion hedge, and its 
relatively low correlation with equities and bonds can make it 
a useful portfolio diversifier. 
In practice, a portfolio concentrated in a single asset class – 
whether Indian equities, USD cash or any other standalone 
exposure – is implicitly relying on one set of economic and 
market conditions remaining favourable. A thoughtfully 
constructed multi-asset portfolio does not need to be the best 
performer in every year, but it is also unlikely to be the worst. 
Its purpose is to build resilience across market cycles: 
capturing growth when conditions are supportive, limiting 
downside when stress emerges, and compounding steadily 
over time in a way that no single asset class can consistently 
achieve on its own.

A practical asset-class toolkit for Global 
Indian investors 
Developed Market (DM) equities 
These are typically shares in large, established companies 
across the US, Europe, the UK and Japan and usually 
represent the largest and most liquid equity markets in the 
world. They are home to the technology giants, global 
healthcare leaders and consumer  brands that dominate their 
sectors globally – many of which have no equivalent in Indian 
indices. DM equities provide the broadest possible access to 
global corporate earnings growth, and for onshore investors, 
they provide USD and other major currency ex posure that 
structurally helps hedge against INR depreciation. For 
offshore investors, DM equities are the natural portfolio core, 
given their base currency and broad exposure of the asset 
class in global markets. 
Emerging Markets (EM) equities  
EM equities span a broad universe of developing economies, 
including China, South Korea, Taiwan, Brazil and other major 
EM markets. They offer greater growth potential than DM 
equities, but also tend to be more volatile. Their returns are 
supported by risi ng consumption, commodity cycles and the 
expansion of industry and the middle class. For Indian 
investors, EM equities provide exposure to regional peers that 
behave differently from Indian domestic markets, adding a 
layer of diversification within the equity sleeve.

1 The building blocks of a Global 
Indian portfolio

### Page 5

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  5

Indian equities  
Indian listed equities can be split up into two distinct sub-asset 
classes: 1) small - and mid -cap equities and 2) large -cap 
equities. The latter represent India’s established blue -chip 
companies with strong balance sheets and have relatively 
lower volatility within the Indian equity spectrum. These are 
typically the anchor of any Indian equity allocation. 
Meanwhile, small - and mid -cap equities tap into a different 
part of India’s growth story: the domestic consumption engine, 
the formalisation of the inform al economy and the 
entrepreneurial mid -market that scales rapidly in a high -
growth environment. They offer meaningfully higher potential 
returns, but also comes with higher volatility and lower 
liquidity, making the appropriate allocation dependent on the 
investor’s risk tolerance and investment time horizon.  
DM Investment Grade bonds 
Developed market investment grade bonds encompass two 
closely related asset classes that play a core defensive role in 
portfolios. Government bonds, such as US Treasuries, 
German Bunds, and UK Gilts, are issued by highly rated 
sovereigns and are widely regarded as the closest equivalent 
to a risk -free asset within global markets. Investment grade 
corporate bonds, issued by financially strong companies rated 
BBB- or above, offer higher yields than government bonds in 
return for accepting a modest amount of credit risk. 
Together, these assets provide an important source of portfolio 
stability. Government bonds have historically performed well 
during periods of economic uncertainty and market turbulence, 
helping to cushion equity losses when investors seek safer 
assets. In vestment grade corporate bonds enhance overall 
income generation while maintaining relatively low volatility, 
allowing investors to improve return potential without taking on 
the higher risks typically associated with equities. 
EM USD bonds 
EM USD bonds are USD -denominated debt issued by EM 
sovereign and quasi-sovereign borrowers, including countries 
such as Indonesia, Mexico, Brazil and South Africa. Their 
USD denomination removes direct exposure to the issuers’ 
local currencies, making them  a more accessible entry point 
into EM fixed income than local -currency bonds, particularly 
for offshore investors with hard -currency liabilities. For 
onshore investors, EM USD bonds add hard -currency fixed-
income exposure and can diversify portfolios conc entrated in 
Indian equities and INR interest -rate risk. However, their 
returns still depend on global interest rates, credit spreads and 
investor risk appetite. 
India government bonds  
The Indian bond market offers something that is increasingly 
rare globally: a genuine positive real (net -of-inflation) yield. 
India’s 10-year government bond yielded around 6.7% in mid-
2026 against a headline inflation of about 4.4%.  This 
represents a positive real yield of nearly 2.8% that compares 
favourably to most DM alternatives. India’s inclusion in global 
bond indices is now also channelling systematic passive 
inflows into this market, providing an additional structural 
demand tailwind.  
Gold 
Gold occupies a unique role in this framework particularly for 
Indian investors. Beyond its universal function as an inflation 
and geopolitical hedge, gold in INR terms has historically 
delivered strong returns during periods of INR weakness 
because gold prices are globally denominated in USD. When 
the INR depreciates, the INR value of gold rises, providing a 
natural cushion against the same currency dynamics that 
erode domestic purchasing power.

The case for global exposure for onshore Indian investors  – Most resident 
Indian investors already diversify across asset classes, typically holding a mix of 
equities, fixed income and, in some cases, commodities such as gold. However, 
this diversification is usually implemented through domestic assets. The gap, 
therefore, is not necessarily a lack of asset-class breadth, but a lack of geographic 
diversification. This is where global investing can play an important role: by adding 
exposure to different economies, currencies, sectors and market cycles  
beyond India.  
Argument

Since India’s market liberalisation in the early 1990s, the INR has depreciated significantly 
against the USD, weakening from approximately INR 31 per USD in 1994 to more than INR 95 
per USD by August 2026 – an annualised depreciation rate of 3.4%. This long-term depreciation 
is not simply a market fluctuation; it reflects a structural feature of an economy that has 
persistently run higher inflation than the US. For investors holding only INR-denominated assets, 
this creates a gradual erosion of global purchasing power over time. USD-denominated foreign 
assets can, therefore, serve as a structural currency hedge, helping preserve international 
purchasing power rather than representing a speculative currency bet. 
The INR 
depreciates 
structurally

Argument

Indian equity indices remain heavily weighted towards the financial, consumer discretionary and 
industrial sectors. By contrast, technology – the largest sector in global DM indices – has a much 
smaller representation in India’s listed market, despite the scale and importance of India’s 
broader technology economy. This gap has become more significant with the rise of artificial 
intelligence (AI). While India has strong technology talent and growing AI adoption, Indian 
markets offer little -to-no exposure to technology in the investible equity market. As a result, 
investors with portfolios concentrated in Indian equities have limited exposure to global 
technology leaders. Adding DM equities, therefore, provides sector diversification, including 
greater partici pation in the growth opportunities associated with AI, as well as geographic 
diversification.

Argument

Indian equities respond sharply to domestic catalysts: RBI rate decisions, monsoon outcomes, 
fiscal borrowing announcements and India -specific political developments. When these factors 
turn negative, they tend to affect domestic equity positions simultane ously. Global equity 
markets are not driven by the same catalysts, so a domestic Indian growth slowdown does not 
cause US corporate earnings to fall or other global equities to retreat. Adding a global equity 
sleeve means that not all equity exposure moves in tandem during a domestic downturn.  
The same logic applies to bonds. Indian bonds respond primarily to RBI monetary policy, 
domestic inflation and India’s fiscal borrowing calendar. These drivers are largely independent 
of the global rate cycle. An investor holding only Indian equities and Indian bonds has all of their 
assets exposed to the same set of domestic macro factors at once. A portfolio that blends Indian 
and global assets reduces this localised cycle dependency, spreading exposure across markets 
that follow different cycles. 
Market cycles are 
not synchronised

The case for Indian asset exposure for offshore Indian investors  – India 
stands out as one of the few major economies where 6 -7% annual real GDP 
growth remains a credible base case through the end of the decade. For offshore 
investors, the allocation case is not built on growth alone, but on four reinforcing 
pillars: a structural growth premium unmatched among large economies; reforms 
that have improved the quality and breadth of that growth; a demographic 
dividend that still has years to run; and a digital public infrastructure revolution 
that is lifting productivity at population scale.

The growth premium  
According to the International Monetary Fund, India remains the world’s fastest-growing major economy. Real 
GDP expanded by 7.6% in 2025 and is projected to grow by 6.5% in 2026 – outpacing China at 4.4%, G7 
economies at 1.6%, and EM and developing economies at 3.9%. This is not a one-year outlier. Apart from the 
Covid-19 pandemic shock, India has sustained a 6 -8% real GDP growth range for much of the past decade, 
reinforcing its structural growth premium. 
This structural growth is also reflected in equity -market performance. Since 2001, Indian equities have 
delivered annualised returns of 10 .1% in USD terms , outperforming DM equities, which returned 7.5% 
annualised over the same period. This long-term track record reinforces the case for including Indian equities 
as a deliberate source of growth within an offshore investor’s portfolio. 
For offshore investors, there is a clear benchmark gap. A global equity benchmark allocation of around 1.3% 
to India materially understates the country’s economic weight. India accounts for roughly 8.2% of global GDP 
on a purchasing power parity basis in 2025, which is significantly higher than its equity index weight. As Indian 
capital markets deepen and foreign ownership constraints ease, this gap between economic weight and index 
representation should continue to narrow, while rising benchmark inclusion can create a recurring source of 
passive inflows into Indian assets.

Economic and market reforms 
India's growth story over the past decade has not just been about the pace of GDP expansion. It also reflects 
a series of market -oriented reforms that have strengthened the investment environment and made growth 
more durable and accessible to long -term cap ital. Examples include the Goods and Services Tax, the 
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, the Real Estate Regulation and Development Act and measures to deepen 
and formalise domestic capital markets. Taken together, these reforms have improved the structural 
competitiveness of the Indian economy in ways that show up in productivity growth, foreign direct investment 
inflows and market depth – not just in headline GDP numbers.

1 Why Indian assets still belong in every 
offshore Indian investor portfolio

### Page 10

InvesTips: Roots and routes – A framework for Global Indian investors  |  September 2026  10

Demographic dividend  
India’s demographic advantage is a long -term structural tailwind, not a short -term population bulge. The 
country has the world’s largest working -age population and, with a median age of around 29.2 (according to 
Worldometer’s latest data), remains one of the youngest major economies. As more Indians enter the formal 
workforce, earn higher incomes and build financial savings, they can support a broader domestic consumption 
base, deeper financial intermediation and continued cap ital-market growth. The shift in household savings 
from physical assets, such as gold and real estate, towards financial assets reinforces this trend. Monthly 
systematic investment plan (SIP) inflows into mutual funds, which have risen from approximately INR 36.6bn 
in 2016 to more than IN R 310bn in 2026, are a clear expression of this demographic and savings transition. 
They also create a durable, domestically -sourced demand base for Indian equities that is less dependent on 
foreign capital flows. 
However, the demographic dividend is not automatic; its realisation will depend on equipping a growing 
workforce with employable, industry -relevant skills and creating sufficient productive jobs. India has made 
meaningful investments in this direction thro ugh the Skill India Mission, including vocational training, 
apprenticeships and courses aligned with emerging sectors. Sustained improvements in training quality, 
employer alignment and job creation will, therefore, be critical to convert India’s demographic scale into lasting 
gains in productivity, income and economic growth.

Digital infrastructure 
India's digital public infrastructure – collectively known as the India Stack – has built a low-cost, inter-operable 
rails system that is accelerating formalisation, financial inclusion and commercial productivity at a speed with 
few global parallels. Exam ples include Aadhaar for identity, the Unified Payments Interface for payments, 
DigiLocker for documentation and the Open Network for Digital Commerce. Together, these platforms are 
productivity infrastructure: reducing the cost of doing business, formalising the informal economy and 
expanding the addressable market for financial services companies, fintechs, consumer platforms and logistics 
businesses in ways that compound into earnings growth over time.

The India fixed income opportunity 
India's 10-year government bond yielded around 6.7% in mid -2026, against headline consumer inflation of 
approximately 4.4% – implying a real yield of around 2.4%. This compares to near-zero or negative real yields 
in much of the developed world over the past decade and to real yields of 1-2% available in most comparable 
EM bond markets.

For onshore Indian investors, adding global assets can improve portfolio 
resilience without diluting expected INR returns. Our modelling shows that moving 
from a fully domestic portfolio to a 15% global allocation lifts the expected return 
only marginally,  from 8.42% to 8.44%, but reduces expected volatility more 
meaningfully, from 18.38% to 16.86%. This improves the risk-adjusted return from 
0.46 to 0.50. The benefit comes from exposure to markets, sectors and currencies 
that do not move in lockstep with I ndian assets, helping reduce domestic 
concentration risk while adding access to different structural growth drivers.

### Page 12

For offshore investors with globally diversified portfolios, Indian assets are best viewed as a deliberate 
strategic tilt towards a differentiated source of long -term growth, rather than as a near -term portfolio 
efficiency trade. Based on our forward -looking Capital Market Assumptions (CMAs), increasing the 
allocation to Indian assets to 15% leaves the expected USD return broadly unchanged at 6.10%, while 
expected volatility rises from 10.32% to 11.00%, reflecting the additional EM and currency risks.  
However, these aggregate estimates may not fully capture the opportunity in Indian financial market assets. 
Our CMAs show that the case for Indian equities is compelling when viewed from a returns perspective 
alone. 
The challenge to portfolio efficiency arises from Indian equities’ relatively high expected volatility. For 
context, our CMAs suggest an expected volatility of 29% for Indian large -cap equities. This number is 
considerably higher than the level experienced over the last 10 years (18.9%) and modestly higher than the 
level over the past several decades. This is a key point because the drag on risk-adjusted returns in Fig. 8 
comes from the higher expected volatility of Indian equities rather than any challenge to the expected return. 
As an example, Fig. 9 illustrates that calibrating Indian equity volatility to more recent market experience 
produces a more balanced assessment of portfolio risk and return. Using recent history as an example 
shows that risk-adjusted returns actually improve modestly from 0.79 to 0.81.  
Overall, this means the debate is less about how high a return Indian equities are expected to deliver over 
the coming years. Instead, how volatile Indian assets are likely to be is key to whether adding them to global 
portfolios improves risk-adjusted returns.

Pulling this together, we would argue that a strong case exists for a measured allocation to Indian assets –
particularly equities – exists, in our view, provided two conditions are met.
First, investors should retain conviction that the structural drivers of India’s return premium – including
earnings growth, improving governance and an expanding investable universe – remain intact. This is key

to being comfortable with the expected return of Indian equities and bonds.
Second, investors must be willing and able to tolerate the volatility associated with Indian equities. While
one could argue that the assumed volatility in our CMAs is high relative to the level experienced in recent
decades, under most scenarios, it would remain meaningfully higher than that of DM equities.
Taken together, offshore Indian investors should consider incorporating Indian assets within a globally

diversified portfolio. However, the allocation should be deliberate and sized in line with their objectives and
risk tolerance, reflecting a conscious investment view and not just the familiarity of a home bias.
